FAITHS OF MEN

A Cyclopaedia of Religions

By the late Major-General J. G. R. FORLONG

F.R.A.S., F.R.S.E., &C.

3 vols., royal 8vo., cloth

1906

Price £5. 5s. net

“ A noble effort to meet a want that is being felt day by day with increasing urgency. . . .
In this object he has admirably succeeded. But he has left us also a monument of a charming
personality. . . . the pioneer movement in a department of scientific inquiry that is of the first
importance to mankind.’’—Dr. T. W. Buys Davids, Journal of Royal Asiatic Society.

“ There are about three thousand articles in these three volumes. . . . The book is not only
erudite, but thoroughly modern, and is simply indispensable for a comprehensive knowledge of
religions. The author was no ordinary man ... in writing this book he has reared a fitting
memorial to a most genial, sincere, and inspiring personality.”—Rev. Dr. J. G lasse, Review of
Theology and Philosophy.

“ It is almost an unique circumstance that one man should have possessed the ability, the
time, the money, and the inclination necessary to carry out such labour ; and the volumes now
published will form a standard source of information for the increasing circle of those who are
interested in the comparative study of human beliefs. . . . the reader of this Cyclopaedia cannot
fail to recognize that the author was a man of truly religious nature, of fine tolerance, and of
earnest desire to learn and maintain truth.”— Blackwood's Magazine, April, 1906.

“ The book is clearly of an erudition immensely wide . . . conveniently arranged for
purposes of reference of facts, and points of recondite learning, concerning religion in general,
and Oriental religions in particular.”— Scotsman, May 5th, 1906.

“ It is altogether a remarkable book, and will be found extremely useful by all students of
religion. . . . This book is the crowning glory of a most industrious, strenuous, and devoted
life.”— Dundee Advertiser, April 12th, 1906.

1 Back (Capt.). Narrative of the Arctic Land Expedition to the month of

the Great Fish River, and along the shores of the Arctic Ocean,

2 BAHAMAS. Tbr ORIGINAL LETTER-BOOK containing (chiefly) Copies

of the Letters which passed between J. HALKETT, Governor of the
Bahamas (, successor to Governor Dow deswell) and LORD HOBART,
Secretary of Slate for the Colonies under the Addington Ministry . The
letters, chiefly written from New Providence, range from Feb. 3rd,
1802 to Jnly 3rd, 1805, are full of valuable details respecting the
Government of that Colony, and the Naval and Military Operations
conducted by Admiral Duckworth against the French, etc., also
references to the unsuccessful Plauters who “ have sent all their Negroes
over to Georgia and some of the other Neighbouring States in America
where they mean to raise cotton." Folio, a Manuscript of 126 leaves;
old oaf 1802-5

'3 Beaujour. Sketch of the United States of North America . . . 1800 to
1810. By le Chev. Felix de Beaujour. Translated by Wm. Walton.
8vo., with folding map; bds. uncut 1814

Autograph letter from the translator inserted.

4 COBBETT (W.). Porcupine’s Works; containing various writings and

selections, exhibiting a faithful picture of the United States of America.
12 vols., 8vo., calf (rebacked) 1801

5 COLUMBUS. Christopher Columbus. His own Book of Privileges, 1502.

Photographic facsimile of the Manuscript in the Archives of the
Foreign Office in Paris, now for the first time published, with expanded
text, translation into English, and an historical introduction. The
transliteration and translation by George F. Bar wick, the introduction
by Henry Harrisse, compiled and edited by Benjamin Franklin Stevens.
8m. folio, boards half covered with pigskin, anchor clasps 1893

An interesting set of documents—the originals being one of the legalised tran¬
scripts which Columbus caused to be made for transmisujn to Qenoa.

D FRANKLIN (Benjamin), m. t. oicero’s cato major, or
his discourse of old-age: With Explanatory notes.
Philadelphia : Printed and Sold by B. franklin, mdccxliv.

Sm. 4tO. A SUPERB COPY IN THE ORIGINAL BOARDS, with
sheep back , entirely uncut ; enclosed in a green morocco
pull-off case 1744 100 0 0

The most celebrated production of Benjamin Franklin’s press.

This copy is in spotless condition ; it measures 8/* x 6£ inches.

The translation was made in 1734 by Chief Justice James Logan,
who was the second of the three famous worthies of Pennsylvania:

Penn, Logan, Franklin. Copies were circulated in manuscript among
friends, and Franklin got one, which he determined to give to the press.

In his preface he calls the book “ this first translation of a Classic in
this Western World” and he wishes it may prove “ a happy omen that
Philadelphia shall become the seat of the American Muses.”

10 GALVAO (Antonio), the | discoveries | of the World

from their | firft originall vnto the | yeere of our Lord |

1555. j Briefly written in the Por- j tugall tongue by
Antonie | Galvano, Gouemour of | Ternate, the chiefe
Ifland | of the Malucos: | Corrected, quoted, and now | pub
lifhed in Englifh by Richard | Hakluyt, fometime ftudent |
of Chriftchurch in | Oxford. | londixi, | Impenfis G. Bifhop.

1601. |

Sm. 4to., black letter, title printed within an ornamental
border; a few leaves a very little wat erstained ; unbd., a
fine, sound copy 1601 35 0 0

One of the barest or Hakluyt’s books ; so rare indeed that the
Hakluyt Society was obliged to make use of a transcript in publishing
the edition which it produced in 1862; just as Hakluyt himself had
been unable to get a sight of the Portuguese original printed at Lisbon
in 1563. If he had succeeded in getting access to that volume, he
would probably have done no more than correct the visible errors of
the English MS. (lying in his hands from about 1588) ; but since he
found that course impracticable, he did better, and carefully investi¬
gated the whole of the matter, leaving us a work which is nearly as
much Hakluyt’s as Galvao’s. He describes the treatise as one which
* l though small in bulke, contaiueth so much rare and profitable matter,
as I know not where to seeko the like ... it was first done into our
language by some honest and well affected marchant of our nation
whose name by no meanes I could attaine vnto.”

55 leaves consisting of 6 preliminary and 97 numbered pages;
with signatures A—N in fours, O three leaves.

14 HERRERA. The General History of the vast Continent

and Islands of America, commonly call’d the West
Indies, from the First Discovery thereof: with the Best
Accounts the People could give of their Antiquities. Col¬
lected from the original Relations sent to the Kings of
Spain. By Antonio De Herrera. . . Translated into
English by Capt. John Stevens. 6 vols., 8vo., with 2
maps and 16 plates , a fine copy in the original calf,
entirely uncut ; very scarce 1725-6

“ No one has ever disputed the fidelity of old Herrera, styled the
Prince of Historians, to the sources of information then accessible,
and no one has ever exceeded him in careful research, and interesting
narration of aboriginal history. He sought and obtained many of the
original documents, which the industry and spirit of the old mission¬
aries and explorers made so numerous and voluminous . . . His work
is a perfect treasure-house of the most valuable details, regarding
the original state of the religion and manners of the Indians. Of
Herrera, Ternaux says, ‘ Among the historians of America this author
holds the first rank.’ ”— Field , Indian Bibliography .

Though possessing some defects, this is the best translation of the
whole of Herrera.

First Edition. Excessivelyrare when in fine condition. Lechford,
exiled for four years to New England for his anti-Episcopalian opinions,
was convinced of their impropriety by the example of many thousands
of Puritan bishops in “ the Bay of the Mattachusets.”

“The author, a lawyer, came to Boston in 1638, where he lived
until It41, and wrote this book, cne of the most interesting and
authentic of the early narratives relating to the colony. It is full of
valuable information respecting the manners and customs of the
colony, and ia written by an able and impartial hand .”—Church
Catalogue.

u . . . The information he gives of the state of the country, civil
and religious, for 1638 to 1641, is valuable enough to render his book
nearly indispensable to the study of New England institutions.”—

J. Hammond Trumbull.

21 LEWIS AND CLARK. Original Journals of the Lewis and Clark

Expedition 1804-6. Printed from the original manuscripts . . . Now
for the first time published in full and exactly as written. Edited,
with Introduction, Notes, and Index, by Reuben Gold Thw^ites.

7 vole, and Atlas of 66 folding maps, being facsimile reproductions
of maps, chiefly by William Clark; now for the first time published.

8 vo., buckram New York 1904-5 12 12 0

The greater part of the original MS. Journals of Meriwether Lewis and William
Clark, herein literally followed, arc in the library of the American Philosophical
Society of Philadelphia.

The Journal of Charles Floyd is published by consent of the State Historical
Society at Wisconsin, in whose library the original manuscript is preserved.

The Journal of Joseph Whitehouse was purchased by the publishers expressly for
the work ; but now is the property of Edward Everett Ayer, Psq , of Chicago.

An admirably scientific work, which may be regarded as almost exhaustive of the

AMERICANA

5

NORDENSKIOLD, continued

subject. Jom&rd and Saotarem might be said to hare produced an imperfect first
volume of an Illustrated History of Cartography treating irregularly upon the manu¬
script sources ; Nordenskiold’s atlas might be called the complete second volume deal¬
ing with the entire printed or engraved material. It is indispensable as furnishing by
far the best apparatus for studying the growth of geographical science, and the gradual
enlargement of knowledge with regard to the surface of the globe.

24 -[Yol. II.] PERI PLUS, an Essay on the early History of Charts

and Sailing-Directions, translated from the Swedish Original by
Francis A. Bather, with nnmerons Reproductions of Old Charts and
Maps. Roy. folio, pp. x and 208, with 60 large maps apart from the
text and 100 maps of smaller size printed in, the text; half morocco
(pub. £10. 10 s) ih. 1897

This second volume deals with the earlier and mainly uaprinted portion of
the subject of Cartography, and gives, for the first time in literature, a comprehensive
view of the early MS. maps and l'ortulani, carried through the Middle Ages and end¬
ing, as a role, with the sixteenth century.

49 -Illustrations of the Botany of Captain Cook’s Voyage round the

World in H.M.S. “Endeavour” in 1768-71. By Sir Joseph Banks
and Dr. Solander. With Determinations by J. Britten. 3 vols., roy.
folio, with 3 maps and 320 plates; bds.

Trustees of the British Museum , 1900-5 4

The Trustees of the British Museum have been pleased to order the copper-plate
engravings of the plants collected daring Captain Cook’s first and second voyages to be
printed. These plates were made at the charge of Sir Joseph Banks bnt were never
published. The manuscript descriptions, which came iuto the possession of the Trustees
together with the plates, are printed verbatim. Mr. Britten has carefully compared
the plates and descriptions with the original drawings and the specimens in the Banksian
Herbai inm and has added such information as these snpply. He has also added
determinations in accordance with the nomenclature at present adopted.

60 BIBLIA POLYGLOTTA ... ex MSS. edidit BRIANUS *

WALTON US, 6 vols.—CASTELLI (Edmundi) Lexicon
Heptaglotta, 2 vols. — together 8 vols., folio, ruled
throughout with red ink; fine copy in old English blue
morocco gilt , edges gilt , with the bookplate of Sir Bobert
Clayton (Lord Mayor of London 1679-80, owner of
Marden in Surrey, M.P. for London, and a man of
distinction in the English political world in the reigns of
Charles II and James III) 1657-69 30 0 0

Born in 1629, Sir Bobert Clayton, who began life without
education, had achieved at his death in 1707 a position of wealth and
honour, which led to the foundation in his successor’s person of a
baronetcy that still flourishes. He was accused in bis own time of
usury and extortion, and of having resorted to unscrupulous measures
in opposition to the Tory party, for which he narrowly escaped the
block.

Dry den mentioned him thus, in M Absalom and Achitopel ” :—

“ Blest times, when Ishban, he whose occupation
So long has been to 6heat, reforms the nation ! ”

Walton’s Polyglot comprises Hebrew, Samaritan, Chaldee, Greek,

Syriac, Arabic, Ethiopic, and Persian, with Latin Vulgate and other
versions.

276 -Plats and Poems. (With Preface and Glossary by Dr. Johnson).

277 -Works, edinbdrgh folio edition. Edited by W. E. Henley,

40 parts, forming 10 vols., folio, with portraits of Shakespeare \ (5),
Jonson , Fletcher, Burbage, Southampton, and Pembroke; boards (pub.
£19. net) 1902-3

This edition is carefully printed with large clear type on hand-made paper..

The editor has kept as close as possible to the text of the first folio edition of
1623. Very few notes are given and the few that are printed are put at the bottom
of the page and are for the most part of a glossarial nature.

This is the most readable edition that has yet been published, and entirely fulfils
the objects Mr. Henley had in view of supplying a good text, printed jin good type on
good paper.

279 SHAKESPEARE. QUARTO FACSIMILES, with introductions, uni-
numbers, etc., bt Shakespeare scholars, issued under the superin¬
tendence or Dr. F. J. Furniyall, Camb., founder and director of the
New Shakespeare Society, by Messrs. W. Griggs, and C. Praetorius.

43 vols., sm. 4to., hf. bd. 1885-91 12 12 0

Separate volumes, with the exception of the fifth, arc sold at 10s 6deach.

Every genuine student of Shakespeare has always desired to own those Quartos of
his Master’s Plays and Poems which are the necessary foundations of the Text of so
many of his greatest productions in the first three periods of his work. Not only in
importance next to the fibst folio, but even above it, for some Plays, and for all the
Poems, stand the first or second quartos, from which, or completed copies of which,
certain Plays in that Folio were printed ; and every trne and faithful worker at
Shakespeare’s text must want to have in his own hand, under his own eye, and as his
owu, trustworthy facsimiles of these truest representations of the poet’s own manuscript.

Till now this baa been practically impossible. Mr. Ashbee’s hand-traced facsimiles,
issued by Mr. Halliwell at five guineas each, could be afforded by only some thirty
subscribers. But what then costive guineas f without any information as to the Quartos
purchased, is now buyable in a new form for 10s 6<f, with the addition of a critical
introduction to the text by a competent Shakespeare scholar.

The New Shakespeare Society at first intended to reprint the best Shakespeare
Quartos, bat gave np its scheme in favour of its founder Mr. Furniyall, who under¬
took to superintend the issue of a series of photo-lithographic Facsimiles of all the most
important Shakespeare Quartos, executed by Mr. W. Griggs, of Elm House, Hanover
Street, Peckham. The Duke of Devonshire, Mr. Alfred ii. Hutb, the Trustees of the
British Museum, the Master and Senior Fellows of Trinity College, Cambridge, and
the Curators of the Bodleian Library, Oxford, allowed their book treasures to be
photographed.

The result is a body of absolutely faithful reproductions, accompanied by critical
remarks npon the texts which are vastly superior to anything else hitherto published in
illustration of the works of Shak«speare.

280 - HAZLITT (W. Carew). SHAKESPEAR: Himself and his

Work. A Study from new points of view. Second edition, revised,
with important additions, and several facsimiles. 8vo., pp. xxxii
and 294; cloth 1903

This volume deals with all the difficult and perplexing problems, which have arisen,
and exist, in connection with the life and productions of Shakespeare—the history of
the “ Sonnets,” the relations between Shakespeare and Bacon, those between the former
and his family, the deer-stealing affair, and the final removal to London—and it
unavoidably conflicts at several points with the statements and opinions of others.
After all the enthusiasm for the version of Montaigne by John Florio, evidence is
adduced that Shakespeare did r.ct resort to it, at all events at first; and this is merely
one instance among many, where the new Essay, which is not a regular biography, but
rather a Biographical Excursion, endeavours to place the private and professional
career of our national poet on a new footing in considerable measure by the collation
of scattered facts and allusions, and the presentment of many circnmstanc?s from fresh
points of view.

(Shakespeare-Bacon Controversy)

Of these extraordinary reproductions that which contains the most curious and
singular matter is the first; the present copy of it is the best and most rare edition,
subsequent ones being so much mutilated as in a great measure to deprive the work of
its peculiar character. The second work is of a description little varying from the
first, but to describe the precise nature of cither, could not with due regard to propriety,
be dono. For some Bibliographical particulars, vide De Bure, No. 856—and for an
admirable Life of the eccentric but learned Author, with an account of the singular
nature of his works, see “ Dictionnaire liistorique, par Chaufepi6,” vol. 1, p. 282.

In the first work a portrait of Beverland, beautifully engraved by I. van Munnik-
hnyse from a painting by T. D. Vols is inserted.

557 -FAITHS OF MAN : a Cyclopaedia of Religions. 3 vols., roy. 8vo.,

pp. xxii, 569, xiii, 582, xiii, and 527, with 3 frontispieces; cloth 1906 5 5 0

This exhaustive Encyclopaedia of Religions is by an author whose previous works
were very well received by the press, and established his reputation as a scholar and a
thinker. But the present work is a great advance on his previous publications, both as
regards the scope, and also the lipe learniug of the writer.

It represents the summing up ,pot only of his own work, but of that of all I he
leading scientific writers on the Fniths or Man during the last thirty years. It is
founded on the reading of 6ome fifteen hundred volumes, including tho l>est authorities
on science and archeology, and the publications of all the leading British Societies.

By the author’s direction the whole has been edited and bronght op to date by an
experienced Orientalist, so as to include the latest archaeological discoveries ; and the
editor in bis preface, which represents a precis of the whole work, states that the subject
is “ illustrated with a fulness of knowledge, a wealth of illustration, and a calmness of
mind, which are probably not to be found combined in any other work. . . . The truly
religions character of the author’s mind will be evident to the reader in all those articles
which deal with Morality, Ethics, and Religion.”

914 NICHOLSON (Edward W. B.). The Vernacular Inscriptions of the

Ancient Kingdom of Alban, transcribed, translated, and explained, by
E. W. B. Nicholson, m.a., Bodley’s Librarian in the University of
Oxford. Sm. 8vo., cloth 1896 0 7 6

These (Pictish) inscriptions (which include the celebrated Newton stone) range
from about the 7th to the late 11th cent. Owing mainly to their phonetic spelling
and to nearly all of them being in the peculiar Ogam alphabet, they were com¬
pletely unsolved till .Mr. Nicholson wrote a series of letters on them in the Academy .

A revised reprint of these letters is included in the volume, which also contains a
classified conspectus of the inscriptions, an exhaustive glossary to them, an essay
on their ages, and a palmographical description.

The volume settles for ever the vexed questions as to what kind of language the
Piets spoke, and whether Highland Gaelic is or is not a dialect derived from the
Scots. It proves that Pictish was nothing more or less than ancient Highland Gaelic,
and that consequently the latter is not a mere offshoot of mediceval Irish.

WELSH TEXTS, continued :— £ *. 0,

947 VII. THE ANCIENT POETRY IN PENIARTH MS. 3, British
Museum Addl. MS. 14,976, etc.

The works enumerated embody the moat noteworthy legacies of the 12th and 13th
centuries in the field of Welsh literature,—in prose, in verse, and in law. 0 Every
volume reproduces in type the main features of its original,—character for character,
line for line, and page for page. Faint letters are underdotted, retracedjetters have a
wavy line under them, and those effaced are underlined, thus informing the reader of the
state of the Manuscript at a glance, as well as dispensing with hundreds of cumbrous
notes. 0 The system adopted makes the Welsh Texts unique among books. The
Series is printed on the best papers, under the personal supervision of the Editor, at his
Private Press: all impressions not up to the best standard will be destroyed. The
outside number printed on Japanese vellum is 150—of these at least 25 will be rejected.

Every copy will be numbered.

Vol. IV of this important historical work is very scarce, and seldom found with
the other three. It contains the general preface, a Monasticon, supplementary matter
with Indexes. It is stated by Mr. Waiter (Southey’s son-in-law) that the stock of the
third volume perished in a great fire at Copenhagen in 1794, and that copies of it were
in consequence very rare ; but his remark probably applied to the fourth, and not the
third, volume.

After many delays the last volume of the Heimskringla is ready for publication.

preceding volumes. The three indexes contain a vast amount of original information
and research, and are of the utmost importance to the student of Scandinavian History.

BERNARD QUARITCH’S CATALOGUE, No. 262

Biasing (Baron Fr. W. von). Denkmaler Agyptischer

Sculptur. To BE COMPLETED in 12 parts, folio, with 144 plates
and numerous illustrations in the text Munich , 1906-8

Subscription price 12 0 0

Parts 1-8 now ready.

A publication has long been wanted by lecturers on the history of Oriental art,
showing in historical and chronological order typical examples of Egyptian art.

Where available copies on the subject exist, they are mostly too small, faulty in
execution, and often difficult of access, through being hidden in books and periodicals
including other subjects. In order to supply such a want this work is now published.

It will be in 144 Plates in chronological sequence, containing a collection of the best
Statues and Reliefs of Egyptian Antiquity, Irom the most ancient times down to the
Roman epoch, copied in large and faithful reproductions from the Originals.

Both the author and publishers have felt it important in choosing the objects for
illustration, not only to give the best known Sculptures, which could not well be
omitted from a comprehensive work, such as this is intended to be, but also to include
a large number of those which have hitherto not been published at all, or only very
imperfectly, and thus to bring them within the reach of students. The text which
accompanies the several plates will not only contain remarks on the origin and present
resting place, state of preservation, size and material of the respective objects, but also
a brief summary of the same as well as notices of the chief parallel bculptures and
literaiy allusions, but excluding such as refer only to their inscriptions. Explanatory
illustrations will be included in the text whenever necessary.

including 20 photogravures; buckram, Norwich (1906)

The importance and utility of a separate work on the Norwich School of Painting*
will be admitted, when it is remembered that the landscape art of Great Britain has
attained its distinguished position by emancipating itself from the trammels of
prescription, and becoming the true offspring of the soil. The potency of locality is
sufficiently illustrated by the remarkable fact that the Folk Lands—Norfolk and
Suffolk—have given us the four gTeat landscape painters—Constable, Cotman,
Crome, and Gainsborough.

Hobson (R. L.). Porcelain: Oriental, Continental, and

Hornby. Ex Bibliotheca Hugh Frederick Hornby.

Catalogue op the Art Library bequeathed by Hugh Frederick
Hornby, Esq., of Liverpool, to the Free Public Library of the City of
Liverpool. Compiled under the direction of the Chief Librarian by
Henry E. Curran and Charles Robertson. 4to. pp. v and 648; art
linen, gilt top Liverpool , 1906

Printed on Dutch Hand-made Paper.

Only a careful study of the Catalogue can reveal the merits of the art books now
in the possession of the City of Liverpool. As a whole, they serve largely to illnstrate
the history and progress of the graphic arts from early times to the present. Of
eighteenth century French illustrated books there is a large and important collection,
showing the works of most of the great book illustrators : Gravelot, Eisen, Marillier,
Moreau, Cochin, &c.: engraved by the first engravers of the time expressly for Editions
de luxe of the great French writers. The great English designers and artists of the
early nineteenth century are well represented, particularly in the case of Stothard,
Westall, and others, together with numerous prints by Bartolozzi, Tomkins,
Schiavonetti, and others of the Bartolozzi school.

This volume is not a merely casual, fugitive, or impressionist sketch of a country
superficially known thiough a hasty journey. The collaborators travelled all over the
land, tarrying in the most fascinating spots ; and while the graphic artist painted, the
literary artist had abundant leisure to study the customs, the costumes, the history, and
the prospects of the people, in whose homes, along with her husband, she was a frequent
and favourite guest.

Taking (Guy Francis). A Catalogue of the Armour and
Arms in the Armoury of the Knights of St. John of
Jerusalem now in the Palace, Yaletta, Malta. 4to., pp. xvii, 52,
with 31 plates; half roxburghe, gilt top 1903 0 10 6

The contents of this famous treasure-house of the Arms and Armour of Medneval
Europe are now perhaps for the first time brought clearly into view, and submitted to
the searching light of keen archaeological inquiry. The collection is of the highest
historical importance, and is of surpassing interest to all antiquarians, recalling, as it
does, the stirring times when the Knights of St. John held the position of an almost
imperial power, and for the space of centuries, by the valour of their achievements,
influenced the destinies of the East and West.

Leighton. Sir Frederic Leighton : An illustrated Chronicle. By

-The Life, Letters, and Work of Frederic Leighton.

By Mrs. Russell Barrington. 2 vols., cr. 4to., pp. xxi, 310, xii, and

407, with 154 plates; cloth 1906 2 2 0

Except by his own family and intimate friends and brother workers, the character
and lifelong aims of Lord Leighton have never hitherto been fully realised. All have
recognised in him the ideal President of the Academy, and a great public has admired
the splendour of his art: beyond this, little that is trustworthy has been kuown of his
life and his intense zeal for the public good—a zeal attested by his wish not to live to
an age when he might cease to benefit others.

The book is illustrated by many of Leighton’s finest works, and by several
drawings and paintings never yet published.

Muther (Richard). The History of Fainting. Translated from

the German, and edited with critical notes, by George Kriehn. 2 yols.,

8vo., with 81 plates; cloth 1906 1 1 0

The author begins with the art of painting in the Middle Ages, and traces its
development through the Italian Renaissance and in the schools of Spain, Holland,

Germany, England, and France to the French Revolution. This work must not be
confused with the same author’s History of Modem Painting , which continues the stndy
to the present time.

Richard Muther is a sound and brilliant critic with a world-wide reputation.

His special aim in this book is to interpret the paintings of the great masters as human
documents and manifestations of the dominant feelings and tendencies of the epochs
to which they belong.

'1 he object of this work is to show how Peerage Law in England—an extremely
interesting branch of English law—now stands, and, so far as material, how it has
stood during the last eight centuries.

The subject is one which appeals alike to the lawyer and to the statesman, to the
student ot history and to the antiquarian : it appeals on personal grounds to those who
are descended (and their number is legion) from ennobled ancestors, and last, but not
least, it appeals to those who possess or hope to inherit or recover an existing Peerage.

This superb example of French Miniature art was written and illuminated after

the death of Saint Louis, to whom it belonged. It is a companion volume to the
Psalter and Honrs of Isabelle of France, six miniatures from which are inclnded as a

obtain a copy of the latter, its necessary complement. Mr. S. C. Cockerell, who has
described the Isabelle Psalter, says : “ It is indeed impossible to treat either of these
inestimable manuscripts without reference to the other, so closely are they related.
That they were written at the same place, and decorated in the same atelier, at
approximately the same time, is obvious. The place was doubtless Paris, the time
between 1253 and 1270.”

Petrarque. Vis de C6sar. Reproduction dn Manuscrit Autographe

Previous to the fortunate discovery by M. Pierre de Nolhac of this important MS.,
only one other of Petrarch’s Latin prose works, the “ De sui ipsins et multorum
ignorami,” was known to be extant in the poet’s autograph. There is every reason to
believe that this was Petraich’s last work, and, indeed, that he was engaged upon it at
the time of his death. As regards the literary value of the work, MM. Kirner and de
Nolhac have shown how mnch it adds to the poet’s glory, it being a precursor of
modern science in the domain of historical and geographical criticism.

Paston (George). Social Caricature in the Eighteenth

Century. Folio, pp. xiv and 144, with coloured frontispiece and
213 plates; hds. 1905 2 2 0

This book, on which George Paston has been working for some jenrs, gives
a general, and as far os possible representative, view of the social caricatures, including
emblematical, satirical, personal, and humorous prints, of the eighteenth century.

Besides Hogarth, Gillray, Rowlandson, and Banbury, who are liberally represented,
characteristic specimens are given of van Heemskerck, J. June, Boitard, George
Bickham, Thomas Patch, Vanderhaechen, Gravelot, Paul Sandby, de Loutherbourg,

John Collett, Samuel Collins, George Woodward, Henry Wigstead, Austin, Isaac
Cruikshanb, and John Kay, the Edinburgh caricaturist. The great majority of these
artist engravers are entirely unknown to the modern public, and their work has not
been represented in any published collection of caricatures. The letterpress includes,
besides notes on the artists, descriptions of the illustrations, and such passages from
contemporary correspondence and periodicals as help to elucidate the subjects treated.

The illustrations include reproductions of line engravings, etchings, mezzotints, stipple,
and a few original drawings by Rowlandson.

Romney. A Biographical and Critical Essay. With a Cata¬
logue Raisonne of his Works. By H. Ward and W. Roberts. 2 yols.,

Limited to 500 copies.

-the same. 2 yols., 4to., beautifully printed throughout in large type on

Japanese vellum ; half blue morocco , gilt tops , uncut 1904

Limited to 350 copies. #

The letterpress is based upon the large and remarkable series of MS. diaries,
notebooks, books of sitters, sketch-books, letters, etc., which were acquired by Mr.
Humphry Ward in 1894, at the sale of the property of the late Miss Romney, grand¬
daughter of the painter.

The diaries cover the twenty central years of Romney’s activity in London, from
1776 to 1795. They are very complete, and contain a mass of information never
before published.

By their aid it has been possible to compile a list of over 2,000 pictures ; and to
trace the dates of sittings, the original cost, and often the first destination of nearly
every portrait.

Many other sources of information have been drawn upon, including Messrs.
Christie’s catalogues and Messrs. Agnew’s private books ; and from these the present
home of most of the more important pictures is given.

Richter (Jean Paul) and A. Cameron Taylor. The Golden
Age of Classic Christian Art [A Study of the Mosaics of
S. Maria Maggiore, Rome]. 4to., with 26 coloured plates and 146 other
illustrations; cloth (pub. at £5. 5 s net ) 1904

The Athenceum for February 11th, 1905, thus concludes a four column review of
this splendid volume: “ This monograph should, we think, have very important
results for the study of early Christian art, and stimulate to a revision of the precon¬
ceived notions of the few monuments that remain to us of the period. It certainly
should throw new light on the extremely interesting and hitherto scarcely explored
problem of decadence in art. It is indeed almost as important to aesthetics, though
scarcely so agreeable to our feelings, to realize how the artistic vision relapses after
a period of maturity, as it is to trace its gradual rise to full powers of expression. The
illustrations in a work of this kind are of the utmost importance, and we can say no
more than that they are worthy of the text.

Smith (David Eugene). Bara Arithmetica. A catalogue of

arithmetics printed before 1601 with a description of those in the
library of GEORGE A. PLIMPTON, Esq. By DAVID EUGENE
SMITH, Professor of Mathematics in Teachers College, Columbia
University. EDITION DE LUXE. 2 vols., roy. 8vo., with 2 coloured
plates , 7 heliotype plates , and 250 illustrations in the text; vellum

Boston , 1908

Limited to 176 numbered sets, of which 150 are for sale.

One of the most valuable features of the book is to be found in the illustrations.
These alone make the work indispensable, not only for public, normal school, and
college libraries, but also for bibliophiles and for students of history and of education.
More than two hundred and fifty facsimiles of pages appear in the work. Many of
these are title pages of rare first editions, but a large number illustrate the development
of arithmetical processes and topics. There are shown, for example, the “ scachiero ”
and “gelosia ” multiplication as set forth in the first printed arithmetic (Treviso, 1478',
the “ galley ” division from this and several other works, the page with the first printed
mention of a slate, sets of figurate numbers from Boethius (in a 1294 manuscript and
the first printed edition), the first square and the first triangular multiplication tables in
print, the first traces of modern long division in manuscript and in print, pages from the
first illustrated arithmetic, the first decimal point and the first scientific treatment ot
decimals, a page from the first arithmetic printed in England, pages of finger numerals,
illustrations of gauging, and the early use of counters in various countries.

Of the full-page plates the following are particularly valuable; a page trom a
Boethius manuscript of 1294, one from a Campanus manuscript of Euclid (earlier than
1260, and presented by Campanus to Urban IV), a curious illustration of a master
teaching the Arab-Hindu numerals in 1444, from a manuscript of Sacrobosco, and a
facsimile of a beautifully written manuscript of about 1450.

A GUIDE TO THE

VARIOUS CLASSES OF DOCUMENTS

PRESERVED IN THE

PUBLIC RECORD OFFICE

By S. R. SCARGILL-BIRD

THIRD EDITION

Roy. 8vo., pp. xxxvii and 460 ; doth 1908

Price 7s.

The Public Records of this oountry have been justly said to excel all others “ in age, beauty,
correctness, and authority.** For a period of well nigh 800 years they contain, in an almost
unbroken chain of evidence, not only the political and constitutional history of the realm and
the most minute particulars with regard to its financial and social progress, but also the
history of the land and of its successive owners from generation to generation, and of the
legal procedure of the country from a time “ whereof the memory of man runneth not to the
contrary.”

Since the earlier editions of the present work were published, in 1891 and 1896 respec¬
tively, very great progress has been made with the systematic arrangement and re-classification
of the contents of the Public Record Office, and the reasons which then appeared to render an
alphabetical arrangement of the Descriptive Catalogue of the Records, under subject-headings,
the moBt desirable form have no longer the same amount of force.

A more scientific grouping of the Records, under the heads of the several Courts or
Offices from which they emanated, has therefore been adopted.

HISTOIRE ROMAINE

DE

TITE-LIVE

REPRODUCTION R^DUITE
DU MANUSCRIT EN ONCIALE, LATIN 5730

de la

BIBLIOTHilQUE NATIONALE

PARIS

[1907]

Among the ancient examples in uncial writing which have been preserved of the Roman
History of Titus Livius the Paris manuscript (latin 67301, which contains almost the entire
text and is the principal source of the third decade of tnis history, has been for long cele¬
brated. Known as the Codex Puteanas from having been formerly in the splendid collection
of MSS. of Claude Dupuy (1545-15941, the Parisian jurist and bibliophile, whose fourth son,
Jacques Dupuy, to whom it had descended, bequeathed it to the king in 1657, this important
text (which dates from the fifth or sixth century) has now been reproduced for the first time
and at a cheap price for the use of the classical scholar and palaeographer.

AUTHORS’ PROSPECTUS

Oor experience in the publication of previous works has convinced us that
such a work could not be properly produced except under the constant
personal supervision of the authors, and in consequence we have decided to
publish it privately.

Having now given five years to its preparation, we have reached a point
at which >ve can say that the completion of our work is assured, and that we
expect to finish it in five volumes, each containing about 200 pages of
letterpress and 60 illustrations.

The remaining two volumes we expect to have ready at intervals of

six months.

The total cost of the work is fixed at £21.
Price of each Volume, £4. 4s net

No volume will be issued except to subscribers to the whole work, but
subscribers may pay for each volume as it appears.

The edition is limited to 500 copies, and a large deman I practically

assured.

THE KOKKA

A MONTHLY JOURNAL OF ORIENTAL ART

Parts 1 — 211 being a complete Set to the end of 1907, folio, with upwards
of 1,000 collotype and chromoxylographic illustrations, in wrappers, u
can be supplied at £56. net

The parts are sold separately as follows :

Parts 1—132 (with the names of the artists and subjects of the plates in
English) can be supplied at 5s. 2d. each and Parts 133-211 at
5s. 9d. each.

Part 182 commenced a purely ENGLISH EDITION of
the Kokka.

Hitherto the English portion of the Kokka had consisted
only in the explanations of plates, all other reading matter
being in Japanese.

Beginning with No. 182 issued in July, 1905, the Kokka
appears in full English garb.

Every number contains superb coloured plates and collo¬
types, faithfully representing treasures of Oriental Art now
preserved in the collections of the noted temples and of
nobles, and other ancient and wealthy families.
